@card extension selection.select
title: Select several commits
place: the timeline
show: none
Selects several commits of the timeline at once, to act on them together.
Entry points: `Ctrl`+click (`Cmd`+click on macOS) on a row of the timeline adds that commit to the selection, or takes it out; `Shift`+click selects every commit between the selected one and the clicked one, both included.
Offered when: in the timeline, wide or narrow. The draft and the rows of stashes are never part of a selection of several.
@card extension selection.bar
title: The bar of a selection of several commits
place: the timeline
show: none
A bar above the commits of the timeline counts the commits selected and offers what is done most with them.
Entry points: shown above the commits of the timeline while two commits or more are selected.
Offered when: two commits or more are selected.
@card extension selection.menu
title: The context menu of a selection of several commits
place: the timeline
show: none
Right-click on a commit of the selection opens the menu of all of them.
Entry points: right-click on any commit of a selection of two or more.
Offered when: two commits or more are selected.
@card extension selection.cherry-pick
title: Cherry-pick several commits
place: the timeline
show: show where
Applies several selected commits onto what is checked out, with one `git cherry-pick`, so that git treats them as one operation to continue, skip or abort.
In VS Code: **Cherry-pick onto `<current>`** in the bar above the graph that counts the selected commits (tooltip *Applies them oldest first, as one operation*) and **Cherry-pick N commits onto `<current>`** in the context menu of the selection, the commits selected with `Ctrl`+click (`Cmd` on macOS) and `Shift`+click (`selectionMenu` in, the bar in). The action `cherryPickMany` runs it with the commits in the order of the rows of the graph, newest first (`engineRuns` in). When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension cherry-pick.commit
title: Cherry-pick a commit
place: the timeline
show: show where
Applies the changes of one commit onto what is checked out, as a new commit, after saying which files will conflict.
In VS Code: **Cherry-pick `<short>`** in the context menu of a commit in the graph and the Gitardo Graph, also on a merge and while an operation is under way (`commitMenu` in); **Cherry-pick `<short>` onto `<current>`** for a commit dropped on the commit checked out or on the row of the uncommitted changes; **Cherry-pick** for a position chosen in the list of the reflog. The action `cherryPick` runs it on the commit. The setting is `gitardo.cherryPick.recordOrigin`. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension revert.commit
title: Revert a commit
place: the timeline
show: show where
Makes a new commit that takes back the changes of a commit.
In VS Code: **Revert `<short>`** in the context menu of a commit in the graph, the Gitardo Graph and the Commits view (`commitMenu`, `logMenu` in), also while an operation is under way. The action `revert` runs it on the commit. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension reset.to-commit
title: Reset the current branch to a commit
place: the timeline
show: show where
Moves the branch checked out (or `HEAD` when detached) to a commit, keeping the changes staged, unstaged, or not at all.
In VS Code: **Reset `<current>` to here…** in the context menu of a commit in the graph and the Gitardo Graph (every commit, the one checked out included), in the Commits view (every commit but the one checked out) and in the list of the reflog; **Reset `<name>` to `<short>`…** for the current branch dropped on another commit. The action `reset` runs it on the commit, and the mode is picked in a quick pick. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension merge.read-predicted-conflicts
title: Predict the conflicts of a merge
place: the timeline
show: none
Works out, without touching the index or the working tree, which files a merge would leave in conflict.
In VS Code: the engine reads it for the merges and the cherry-pick it runs; the extension does not read it.
@card extension merge.into-current
title: Merge into the current branch
place: the timeline
show: show where
Merges a commit, branch or remote branch into what is checked out, as the configuration of git says: a fast-forward when one is possible, otherwise a merge commit.
In VS Code: **Merge into `<current>`** in the context menu of a commit, of a local branch (its label in the graph, the references, the Branches view) and of a remote branch, also while an operation is under way, and **Merge `<name>` into `<current>`** in the menu of a branch dropped on the commit checked out, not while one is (`mergeEntries` and `integrate` in). The action `merge` with no way, or the way `default`, runs it (`engineRuns` in); when it stops on conflicts, the message is *Merge `<name>` into `<current>` stopped: N files have conflicts. Resolve them, then continue or abort the merge.* (`reportEngineFailure` in). When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension merge.choose-way
title: Merge into the current branch, choosing how
place: the timeline
show: show where
The entry after **Merge into `<current branch>`** asks which of the four ways to merge, as the menu beside **Pull** has the ways to pull.
In VS Code: **Merge into `<current>`…**, with the hint *choose how*, right after **Merge into `<current>`** in the same menus, and **Merge `<name>` into `<current>`…** in the drop menu. The action `merge` with the way `ask` runs it, and the way is picked in a quick pick; a view that names the way gives it as the answer `way` (`noFastForward` as `no-ff`, `fastForwardOnly` as `ff-only`, `squash`). A fast-forward only that is not possible is refused with a warning. After a squash, the message has no buttons, and the commit box of the Changes view gets the message git prepared (the follow-up `preparedMessage`). When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension merge.undo-squash

A squash merge stages changes and moves no branch, so the reflog cannot take it back: this takes what it staged out of the index and the working tree.
In VS Code: **Undo** in the toolbar right after a squash merge, which `undo.last` finds in the journal. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.

@card extension rebase.find-line-before-head
title: The line of commits an interactive rebase can rewrite
place: the timeline
show: none
The commits that an interactive rebase starting at a commit would make again: from `HEAD` down to that commit, when they form a single line without merges. It decides where the interactive rebase, **Edit message…**, **Split this commit…**, **Squash…** and the fixup are offered.
In VS Code: in the views, and of decide what the menus offer: `canReword` is false on the row of the uncommitted changes and during an operation or a bisect, and true on `HEAD`, a merge or not. The engine works it out again for the feature it runs.
@card extension rebase.read-pushed
title: Where a commit was pushed
place: the timeline
show: none
The remote branches that have a commit, read before a commit is rewritten, so that the confirmation can say the branch will have to be force pushed.
In VS Code: the engine reads it for `message.edit`, `squash.commits` and `split.commit`, not for the fixup, the rebase editor, `rebase.onto` or the reset; the extension does not read it.
@card extension rebase.read-stacked-branches

The local branches, other than the one rewritten, whose tip is one of the commits a rebase is about to make again: the branches of a stack, each started from the one under it, which the rebase would leave on the old commits.
In VS Code: the engine reads it for the operations it runs; the extension does not read it.
@card extension rebase.update-refs
title: Which branches move with a rewrite
place: the timeline

Decides whether a rewrite takes the other branches of a stack with it, and says which in its plan, so that a branch on a commit made again is not left on the old one.
In VS Code: the engine's, in the plans of those operations, which the extension runs on the engine; the setting is `gitardo.rebase.updateRefs`.
@card extension rebase.todo-page

The list of the commits from a commit up to `HEAD`, to reorder by dragging and to pick, reword, edit, squash, fixup or drop each one before the rebase starts.
In VS Code: a dialog over the Graph view, `RebaseEditor`, opened by **Interactive rebase of N commits…** or **Edit this commit (interactive rebase)…** in the context menu of a commit in the graph or the Gitardo Graph. **Start rebase** posts the action `rebaseInteractive` with the steps oldest first, the base, and each subject reworded with `keepBody`.
@card extension rebase.build-todo-list

In VS Code: `todoRows` writes the steps of the rebase editor as the rows of `rebase.interactive`: `<action> <full hash>`, and for a reword its new subject, from which the engine builds this list.
@card extension rebase.interactive
title: Run an interactive rebase
place: the timeline
show: show where
Runs `git rebase -i` with the todo list prepared on the interactive rebase page, by having git's editor of the todo list copy the prepared one over it, since no editor can be typed into.
Entry points: **Start rebase** on the interactive rebase page (`rebase.todo-page`). The same plan is what `message.edit`, `squash.commits`, `squash.into-previous` and `split.commit` build for an older commit.
Offered when: as `rebase.todo-page`.

Its rows, buttons and context menu are those of this list, the operations sent to Gitardo's engine as their cards say; a file of a stash opens in VS Code's diff editor. @card extension stash.push

@card extension stash.preview
title: What applying a stash would stop on
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: none
Says, before a stash is applied or popped, what git would stop on: the conflicts its changes make with what is committed, the changed files it would refuse to touch, and the untracked files it took that are there again.
In VS Code: the engine's plans of `stash.apply` and `stash.pop` carry it, and the extension shows it in the note of their modal dialog (`VscodeAsker` in): files in conflict count as files with changes, and nothing is said of conflicts for a stash git will refuse. @card extension stash.apply
title: Apply a stash
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: show where
Brings the changes of a stash back into the files, and keeps the stash.
In VS Code: **Apply** in the context menu of a stash in the Stashes view, in the Stashes section of the references beside the graph and on its row in the graph; the first button on the row of a stash in the Stashes view (tooltip **Apply: bring the changes back and keep the stash**). Each sends `feature/run` of `stash.apply` to Gitardo's engine, the stash by its index in `stash@{N}` (`stashIndex` and `engineRuns` in, run by), its plan always shown in a modal dialog first (`VscodeAsker`). A name that is not `stash@{N}` is not sent to the engine, and nothing runs. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension stash.pop

Each sends `feature/run` of `stash.drop` to Gitardo's engine, the stash by its index (`engineRuns` in, run by), its warning always asked in a modal warning dialog (`VscodeAsker`); the engine's journal keeps what `stash.undo-drop` needs, and **Undo** is the engine's (`undo.last`). @card extension stash.autostash-left
title: The stash a pull leaves
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: none
A pull with `--autostash` sets the changes aside, pulls, and puts them back; when they no longer fit on what came in, git leaves them in conflict in the files and keeps the stash. Nothing says so unless git's output is read: Gitardo says it, and offers the stashes.
Entry points: after `sync.pull` with `--autostash`, **Pull** or another way to pull in the menu of **Sync**.
@card extension conflicts.banner
title: The banner of an operation that stopped
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Says that an operation is waiting for the user, how many files are in conflict, and gives the ways on, back and to the resolutions, at the top of the timeline and of the Changes page.
In VS Code: `OperationBanner` in, above the graph in the panel and in its editor tab (not in the Gitardo Graph of the side bar, where the Changes view right above says it) and at the top of the Changes view, with for the count. What it shows is read by the engine: the operation and the stop of a rebase come with the state (`repo.read-state`, through `reads.parts` in), and the counts with `conflicts.resolve-by-rules` when `gitardo.conflicts.autoResolve` is on. The settings are `gitardo.conflicts.autoResolve` (the count and the wand button) and `gitardo.conflicts.ai` (the sparkle button). **Continue**, **Skip** and **Abort** run in Gitardo's engine, as their cards say; the wand and the sparkle open the review.
@card extension conflicts.continue-operation
title: Continue
place: the Conflicts page
show: quick search
Goes on with the operation that stopped, once its conflicts are resolved and staged.
In VS Code: **Continue** in the banner of the graph and of the Changes view, **Continue** in the message that follows `conflicts.resolve-automatically` when no file is left in conflict, and **Continue the merge** in the report of `review.write` and on the empty review. It sends `feature/run` of `conflicts.continue-operation` to Gitardo's engine (in, `engineRuns` in), at a stop to edit or to split a commit too: the engine goes on as the stop says, as the rewriting area has it. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension conflicts.skip-step
title: Skip
place: the Conflicts page
show: quick search
Leaves out the commit or patch the operation stopped on and goes on with the next one.
In VS Code: **Skip** in the banner sends `feature/run` of `conflicts.skip-step` to Gitardo's engine (in, `engineRuns` in), whose question is asked through `VscodeAsker` in. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension conflicts.abort-operation
title: Abort
place: the Conflicts page
show: quick search
Takes the repository back to how it was before the operation started.
In VS Code: **Abort** in the banner sends `feature/run` of `conflicts.abort-operation` to Gitardo's engine (in, `engineRuns` in), whose warning is asked through `VscodeAsker` in. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension conflicts.stopped
title: The message when an operation stops on conflicts
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Says that an operation stopped on conflicts, what to do next, and offers the resolutions at once.
In VS Code: in, shown by when a step of the engine fails and, once the repository is read again, a file is in conflict. It is a VS Code warning message whose buttons are **Resolve Automatically**, **Resolve with AI** and **Show Log**; the settings are `gitardo.conflicts.autoResolve` and `gitardo.conflicts.ai`.
@card extension conflicts.read-files
title: The files in conflict and their sides
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Which files are in conflict, and which of the three versions git keeps in the index each of them has.
In VS Code: read by the engine: and in, and and in, which take the sides from the engine (`reads.conflictVersions`, `merge-view.read-sides`) and the file on disk for the plans. Each read of the repository counts what the rules resolve only while `gitardo.conflicts.autoResolve` is on (`reads.parts`, with `conflicts.resolve-by-rules`); the review reads the plans whenever it is open (`ConflictReviewTab.read` in). The limits are those of `conflicts.resolve-by-rules` (`CONFLICT_BYTES`, `CONFLICT_FILES`).
@card extension conflicts.side-names
title: What the two sides are
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
The words that say what **Current** and **Incoming** are in the operation that stopped, the branch or commit each one is, and the subject of its commit. The Merge page, the review page, the questions to the AI provider and the questions of `conflicts.take-side` all say the sides this way.
In VS Code: `CONFLICT_SIDES` in for the words, with the names and the subjects read by the engine (in, `read/conflicts.side-names`). They name the sides in VS Code's merge editor, in the diff of a file one side deleted and in the review; the questions of `conflicts.take-side` and to the language model are the engine's.
@card extension conflicts.open-file
title: Open a file in conflict
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Opens a file in conflict where it can be resolved by hand, which depends on what the two sides did to it.
In VS Code: a click on a file of the **Conflicts** group of the Changes view (in), the diff button of `review.deleted-card`, and the line of a card in the review. A file with stages 1, 2 and 3 opens in VS Code's merge editor (in, VS Code's internal `_open.mergeEditor` command), titled **Merging: \<name\>**, with the base from `:1`, **Current** from `:2` and **Incoming** from `:3`; never the Flow Merge, which is reached from the button of the merge editor. A file one side deleted opens in VS Code's diff editor, `:1:<file>` on the left and the file on disk on the right; any other file in a plain editor, with its markers. The stages are read by the engine (`reads.conflictStages` in).

@card extension conflicts.take-side
title: Take the current side, take the incoming side
place: the Conflicts page
show: show where
Resolves a whole file for one side when that side is simply right: the file becomes what that side has, or is deleted when that side deleted it.
In VS Code: the buttons of a file in the **Conflicts** group of the Changes view, with the same tooltips, and **Keep it, with these changes** and **Delete it** on `review.deleted-card`. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension conflicts.parse-markers
title: The conflicts git marked in a file
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Finds the conflicts in the text of a file as git marked them, in any of git's styles (`merge`, `diff3`, `zdiff3`), and whatever names git wrote after the markers.
In VS Code: , `marks` and in, in TypeScript, for the review and the Flow Merge, which stay in the extension; the counts of the banner are the engine's (`conflicts.resolve-by-rules`). `resolveConflicts` returns no conflict when the markers do not pair up.
@card extension conflicts.resolve-by-rules
title: The conflicts that need no choice
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Resolves the conflicts of a file that need no choice between the two sides, and says why each one could be resolved. Git stops wherever the two sides changed lines that touch; many of those stops are two changes side by side, the same change twice, or the same lines spaced differently. Every line it writes is a line of one of the two sides, as it is in the file: it never makes one up. Anything that is not plainly one of its cases is left exactly as git marked it.
In VS Code: and in, in TypeScript (`weave`, `refine`, `mergeWith`, `settle`, `plan`, `MAX_LINES`, `MAX_EDITS`), with the cases `separate`, `same`, `whitespace` and `oneSide` of `Resolution` and their words in `RESOLUTIONS` of. They propose in the review, on the sides the engine reads (`conflictPlans` in); the counts of the banner are the engine's (`read/conflicts.resolve-by-rules`, through `reads.parts`), and what is written from them is written by the engine (`conflicts.resolve-automatically`, `review.write`).
@card extension conflicts.resolve-automatically
title: Resolve conflicts automatically
place: the Conflicts page
show: quick search
Writes the resolutions of `conflicts.resolve-by-rules` in the files at once, after saying what they are, with a way to look at each one first.
In VS Code: **Gitardo: Resolve Conflicts Automatically** in the Command Palette (shown while `gitardo.conflicts.autoResolve` is on) and **Resolve Automatically** in the message of `conflicts.stopped`. The check of `gitardo.conflicts.autoResolve` stays in TypeScript, at the start of, and refuses with **Gitardo: resolving conflicts automatically is experimental, and it is switched off in the settings.** and **Open Settings**; then it sends `feature/run` of `conflicts.resolve-automatically` to Gitardo's engine (in, `engineRuns` in). Its question, with a detail of several lines and the two answers **Resolve** and **Review**, is a modal of VS Code through `VscodeAsker` in; **Review** opens the review (the follow-up `openReview`), and the message after it is one message with **Continue**, when it offers it, and **Undo**. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension conflicts.restore
title: Undo a resolution
place: the Conflicts page
show: show where
Marks the conflicts of files again as git had marked them when it stopped, after Gitardo wrote resolutions in them or took one side of them.
In VS Code: **Undo** in the toolbar, in the message that follows `conflicts.resolve-automatically` and in the report of `review.write`. Undo is `undo.last` in the engine, whose journal records `conflictsResolved` for the files written or the side taken. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension review.page
title: The review of the resolutions
place: the Conflicts page
show: page
A page with every conflict of the files in conflict, one card each, where what is proposed for each one is looked at, accepted or not, and written. Nothing is written until the user writes what was accepted.
In VS Code: an editor tab, in, with the host side `ConflictReviewTab` in, opened by; its data is made by in. It is opened by **Gitardo: Review the Resolutions of the Conflicts** and **Gitardo: Resolve Conflicts with AI** in the Command Palette, the wand and sparkle buttons of the banner and of a file in the Changes view, **Review** in the question of `conflicts.resolve-automatically`, and **Resolve with AI** in the message of `conflicts.stopped`. The review and its proposals stay in the extension, in TypeScript; what it reads is read by the engine (`ConflictReviewTab.read`, with `reads` of and `conflictPlans` of), and `review.write` runs in the engine. It is read again whenever the repository is read again, the settings of `gitardo.conflicts` change, or the models of VS Code change.

@card extension ai.resolve-conflicts
title: Resolve conflicts with AI
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Asks the AI provider how to resolve each conflict the rules leave, one conflict per question, and shows each answer as a proposal to accept or not.
In VS Code: asks the language model chosen in VS Code, not an AI provider:, `next` and `stop` in (`AT_ONCE` = 2, `SEND_EVERY` = 120 ms); the answers are kept in while VS Code runs. The review stays in the extension; each question is `ai.ask-conflict` of the engine (`askOnEngine` in), which `answerLanguageModel` in sends to VS Code's model, and the answer shows as it comes. Its entry points add **Gitardo: Resolve Conflicts with AI** in the Command Palette and the sparkle on a file in the Changes view, tooltip **Resolve with AI, after looking at what is proposed**. The settings are `gitardo.conflicts.ai`, `gitardo.conflicts.aiModel` and `gitardo.conflicts.aiEffort`; with the first off it says **Gitardo: resolving conflicts with AI is experimental, and it is switched off in the settings.** with **Open Settings**.
@card extension ai.ask-conflict
title: One question about one conflict
place: the Conflicts page
show: none
Sends the AI provider the question about one conflict and returns its answer as it comes.
In VS Code: `read/ai.ask-conflict` of the engine, asked by the review with the file's lines and the conflict: the engine reads the names of the sides, writes the question, and asks it with `host/languageModel`, which `answerLanguageModel` in sends to a language model of VS Code: the one of `gitardo.conflicts.aiModel`, or the first, with `reasoningEffort` set to `gitardo.conflicts.aiEffort` when it is set, the text shown in the review as it comes; then it reads and trims the answer. The request carries the justification **Gitardo sends the conflicts of the merge, and the lines around them, to the model to propose how to resolve them.** A model that is not there fails with *The model chosen to resolve conflicts, \<id\>, is not available in VS Code: choose another one.*

Narrows the timeline to the commits that changed a file, under every name it had. The graph stays connected, so it still shows on which branches the file changed.
Entry points: **Show file history** in the context menu of a file in the Files page, of the File page and of its title; the history button (a clock with an arrow turning back) next to each file in the inspector; **History of the active file** in the **…** menu of the header; **Show file history** in Quick search, for the File page in front.
Offered when: the file is inside a repository of the workspace. In a workspace with several, the repository that holds the file (the innermost one) is shown first.
Refuses: a file outside every repository of the workspace, *Open a file of this repository to see its history.*
@card extension file-history.read-committed-lines
title: The lines as the commit checked out has them
place: the Files page
show: none
Before following lines back, they are found in the commit checked out, since git follows them from a commit, while the file in the File page may have edits not saved or not committed.
Entry points: none: used by `file-history.lines`.
@card extension file-history.map-lines
title: Map lines of a file as it is to the file as committed
place: the Files page
show: none
Finds which lines of the file as committed some lines of the file as it is now come from.
@card extension file-history.lines
title: Show the history of some lines
place: the Files page
show: quick search
Narrows the timeline to the commits that changed the lines selected in the File page, or the line of the cursor (`git log -L`). Git follows the lines back from the commit checked out, through moves too.
Entry points: **Show history of selected lines** in the context menu of the File page and in Quick search.
Offered when: the File page in front shows a file on disk inside a repository.
Refuses: - no File page of a file of a repository in front: *Open a file of this repository to see the history of its lines.* - the file is in no commit: *`<file name>` is in no commit yet, so its lines have no history.* - every line selected was added since: *This line was added after the last commit, and has no history yet.* or *These lines were added after the last commit, and have no history yet.*
@card extension file-history.folder
title: Show the history of a folder
place: the Files page
show: show where
Narrows the timeline to the commits that changed anything in a folder.
Entry points: **Show folder history** in the context menu of a folder in the Files page.
Offered when: the folder is inside a repository and is not the root folder of a repository, whose history is all of it.
Refuses: a folder outside every repository, *Choose a folder of this repository to see its history.*
@card extension blame.read-line
title: Who last changed one line
place: the Files page
show: none
Entry points: none: used by `blame.current-line`.
@card extension blame.current-line
title: The blame of the line under the cursor
place: the Files page
show: none
The line under the cursor in the File page shows who last changed it, when, and the subject of that commit, dimmed at the end of the line.
In VS Code: `BlameAnnotator`, a decoration after the end of the line in the editor, `blame.read-line` asked through `reads.blameLine`. The answers are kept by `LineBlames` of for the document, its version and the line, and asked again only when one of them changes: moving the cursor along a line, or back to a line of the same text, asks nothing, and the text not saved is sent once for each version; they are forgotten when the document is closed, `HEAD` moved or `blame.ignoreWhitespaceAndMoves` changed; tested by and by the step of that moves the cursor along a line and counts no read.

Entry points: **Toggle inline blame** in Quick search.

Entry points: `Alt+B` (`Ctrl+Alt+B` on macOS) in the File page; **Open commit of current line as a page** in the context menu of the File page and in Quick search.

Refuses: no File page of a file of a repository, *Put the cursor on a line of a file of a repository.*; a line not committed, *This line is not committed yet.*; a line git has no blame for, *This line is in no commit.*

@card extension blame.file
title: The blame beside the lines of a file
place: the Files page
show: none
Beside each line of the File page, before its text, the day it was last changed and by whom, with the commit's other lines brought out and the age of each line in color.
In VS Code: `FileBlame`, drawn with decorations of the editor over the room an inlay hint keeps before each line. An edit draws again only the editors of the file typed in; the lines of the commit under the cursor are a decoration of their own over the column, so that moving the cursor to another commit draws those lines and not every line. `refresh` reads again the files of a repository whose `HEAD` moved, as `HeadsSeen` of tells from the state read, not at every state, and a file as it was in a commit not at all. Tested by the steps of the blame of a file in.

@card extension blame.previous
title: Blame the file as it was before a commit
place: the Files page

Opens the file as the commit that last changed a line found it, with the blame it had then: who had written what the commit replaced. Going on from there walks back through the history of a line, past the commits that only moved or reformatted it.
Entry points: **Blame the file as it was before** in the hover of the blame of a line (`blame.file`); **Blame file before commit of current line** in the context menu of the File page and in Quick search.

Refuses: as `blame.open-line-commit`; and when the commit of the line added the file, *`<short hash>` is the commit that added this file, so there is nothing before it to blame.*

@card extension worktree.create
title: Create a worktree
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: quick search
Makes a new worktree on a new branch, from a branch the user picks, in the folder of the worktrees.
In VS Code: **+** on the title of the Worktrees section, among the references beside the graph and in the Branches view; **Create worktree…** in its **…**; **Gitardo: Create Worktree…** (`gitardo.createWorktree`) in the Command Palette and in the **…** of the Branches view; the last entry of **Gitardo: Switch Worktree…**. Each sends `feature/run` of `worktree.create` on `HEAD` to Gitardo's engine (`worktreeAdd` with no `ref` in `engineRuns` of, run by in); the questions are asked in VS Code's pickers and input boxes, the name checked by the engine with `question/validate` as it is typed (`VscodeAsker` in). The format and its inputs are `gitardo.branches.format` and `gitardo.branches.inputs`, or, where they are not set, `gitardo.worktrees.branchFormat` and `gitardo.worktrees.inputs`, sent by. What follows the worktree made is the extension's: the engine hands the commands of `worktree-setup.command-steps` over in `waiting`, and the extension runs them in VS Code's terminals; once they have all succeeded, the message **Worktree created in `<folder>`** has **Open in Agents Window** (where VS Code has it) and **Open in New Window** in place of the desktop's three, and **Choose What It Gets…** while the engine offers it, once for the workspace (workspace state `SETUP_OFFERED`). When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.

@card extension worktree.locked
title: What a locked worktree shows, and what it changes
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: none
How a lock is read and shown, and what **Remove…**, **Rename…**, **Prune** and the clean-up of branches do with a locked worktree, which git refuses to remove or move and never prunes.
In VS Code: the same rules, in the engine; the rows of the Worktrees section draw the lock after the label, say `missing, locked`, and their tooltips say the reason (`lockTitle` in). The rows of the **Gitardo Repositories** view do not draw the lock yet.
@card extension worktree.read-folder-links
title: Read the links to folders outside a worktree
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: none
On Windows, git deletes what is inside a junction when it removes a worktree: the folder it links to, in the folder the link came from. Elsewhere, a symbolic link that a pattern for folders such as `node_modules/` does not ignore is a file git does not track, and `git worktree remove` refuses the worktree. This read finds those links, so that they are taken out first.
In VS Code: read by the engine, in the plan of `worktree.remove`, which the extension runs there.
@card extension worktree.recover-held-folder
title: Get past a folder that Windows holds
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: none
On Windows another program can hold a worktree's folder for a moment, such as a window or a terminal that has just left it. A move or a removal that fails for that is not left half done.
In VS Code: carried out by the engine in the plans of `worktree.rename` and `worktree.remove` the extension runs there, after had VS Code's Git extension close the worktree, which is what holds it most often inside VS Code.
@card extension worktree.remove-empty-folders

In VS Code: steps of the engine's plans of `worktree.rename`, right after `git worktree move`, and of `worktree.remove`, right after `git worktree remove`, both before git touches the branch.

@card extension worktree-setup.command-steps
title: Commands in a new worktree
place: Kept aside, in the branch rail
show: none
The commands of `worktrees.setupCommands`, such as `npm ci`, run in a new worktree once it has been created and its links and copies made.
In VS Code: the commands are `gitardo.worktrees.setupCommands`, set in the workspace folder, the workspace file or the user's settings. The extension says `host.terminal` when it starts the engine, so the engine hands the `Task` steps over in `waiting` instead of running them, and runs them with `runSetupCommands` of, without waiting for them: one after the other, each as a VS Code task named after the folder of the worktree (source *Gitardo*), a shell execution with the worktree as its folder, in a terminal of this window shown without taking the focus, the next only once the last ended with exit code 0. Trust is VS Code's: the extension sends `trust` `vscode`, so the engine asks no trust question, and the commands run only in a trusted workspace. A command that fails, or a workspace not trusted, is said in a warning with **Open in Agents Window** (where VS Code has it) and **Open in New Window**. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.

@card extension submodule.deinitialize

show: quick search

In VS Code: built: **Save as archive…** in the context menu of a commit, a branch, a remote branch or a tag in the graph and in the Branches view, the questions in VS Code's pickers and its save dialog, sending `feature/run` of `archive.save` to the engine.

Done by VS Code itself.
@card extension undo.last
title: Undo the last operation
place: the header and the bottom panel
show: quick search
**Undo** takes back the newest operation of Gitardo, as its journal keeps it: a commit, amend, checkout, merge, pull, rebase or reset by the reflog, and what the reflog does not see from the journal itself. With nothing in the journal, after a commit made in a terminal for one, it takes back the last move of the reflog. When the newest operation is one it cannot take back, a push, a fetch, a tag, a stash or a worktree, it says so rather than take back the one before it. It always shows what it is about to do first. With nothing in the journal, undoing twice redoes.
In VS Code: **Undo** in the toolbar of the graph, whose tooltip names the newest operation of the journal (*Undo "Commit"*, or *"Push main to origin" cannot be undone*, from `journal/last`, which checks the journal against the state of the last `repo.read-state` instead of reading the repository again, and reads it only when no state was read since the last operation (M15)); **Gitardo: Undo Last Operation** in the Command Palette and in the **…** of the headers of the Commits view and the Gitardo Graph; the **Undo** of the message after resolving conflicts automatically. Each sends `feature/run` of `undo.last` to Gitardo's engine (in), its plan always shown in a modal dialog first (`VscodeAsker` in). Every operation is the engine's, so its journal has them all. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so.
@card extension undo.journal
title: Remember what Undo needs
place: the header and the bottom panel
show: none
The engine keeps, for each repository, a journal of Gitardo's operations, newest last, each with what Undo needs of it: a move of `HEAD` the reflog takes back, the commit a branch was at, a branch created, what was checked out before a branch with no history (`branch.create-orphan`, whose move git writes in no reflog), the commit of a stash, the files whose conflicts were resolved, the `HEAD` before a series, or why it cannot be taken back. It holds while `HEAD` is what the newest entry left, and is emptied once `HEAD` moves by something Gitardo did not do. An operation that cannot be taken back and changed only files and the index (staging, a save, a discard) adds nothing, and Undo goes past it. It is kept in memory: a new engine starts with an empty journal, and Undo then goes by the reflog.
@card extension undo.moved-branches
title: Take back the branches a rewrite moved
place: the header and the bottom panel
show: none
The journal entry of a rewrite that moved the other branches of a stack keeps where each was, so that one Undo puts them back with the branch rewritten.
In VS Code: the engine's, in the plan of `undo.last` that the extension runs on the engine.
@card extension activity.track
title: Say what is running
place: the header and the bottom panel
show: none
Every read, plan, comparison and request that may take longer than a frame is registered while it runs, with what it is in words, when it started and what asked for it, so that the activity indicator of the status bar (`activity.status-bar`) and the **Activity** tab (`activity.what-is-running`) can say it. The words are those of `activity.describe-task`.
@card extension diff.page
title: The Diff page
place: the Diff and Merge pages
show: page
Two versions of a file side by side, kept level, with every change joined across the gutter to where it lands.
Entry points: a click on a file of the changes unless `changes.openOnClick` is `file`, and **Open changes** of the menu of its row; a file in the inspector, beside it (`diff.beside-inspector`); a file in the Commit page and in a comparison; **Compare with the saved file** in the Changes page; **Compare with…** on a file; the button on the header of the Changes page that opens all its diffs in one page; **Show the result** in the review of the conflicts; `conflicts.open-file` for a file deleted on one side; a deleted file of the Files page (`file-view.files-changes`).
Refuses: a file with a NUL byte on either side: *This file looks binary.* A diff that takes more than 5 seconds to compute: *This file has too many differences to display.*

@card extension diff.stage-change
title: Stage this change
place: the Diff and Merge pages
show: show where
Puts one change of the working tree into the index.
In VS Code: the stage button in the gutter of the Flow Diff on a change, offered while the Flow Diff shows a file against `HEAD` or the index without a rename (`canStage` in). `ChunkActions` in sends `diff.stage-change` to the engine with a `diffChange` target (`changeTarget`: the path, the two sides, the lines of the change on each and their text), through the `runOp` the Flow Diff is given, which is and so. The engine checks the change against the file on disk: a file with unsaved edits in VS Code is refused with *The file changed since the diff was shown. Try again.*, where the extension used to stage against the text in the editor. When the engine's binary is missing, it says so. Ranges staged in VS Code's own diff editor (**Stage Selected Ranges**) are VS Code's, not Gitardo's: the Changes view shows their result.
@card extension diff.unstage-change

place: the Diff and Merge pages
show: show where
Takes one change back out of the index.
In VS Code: the unstage button in the gutter of the Flow Diff on a change already staged, drawn dimmed: as `diff.stage-change` says, `ChunkActions` sends `diff.unstage-change` with a `diffChange` target through, and the engine refuses a change of a file with unsaved edits; a change of a file that `HEAD` does not have is unstaged whole, `git restore --staged -- <file>`.

show: none
The content a pointer stands for, read from the store of Git LFS on this computer, never downloaded by a read.
In VS Code: built: `read/lfs.read-content` (protocol 1.13) gives a file of a commit, or of the index for no revision, with the content in place of its pointer when the store has it, the pointer with its size, its id and `isDownloaded` otherwise; the files of the scheme `gitardo` the extension opens, the sides of its diffs among them, are read with it (`reads.fileShownAt`).
@card extension lfs.show-in-pages
title: A file stored with Git LFS, in the pages
place: the Files page
show: none
What the File page, the Diff page and the page of a commit show of a file stored with Git LFS: its content when it is on this computer, and otherwise that it is not downloaded, with **Download**, never the text of its pointer.
In VS Code: built: a version of a commit or of the index that is a pointer opens as its content when it is in the store, and otherwise as a text that says **Stored with Git LFS, not downloaded**, with the path, the size and the first ten digits of its id, never the lines of its pointer; a message of VS Code says the file is not downloaded, with its size, and offers **Download**, once for each version opened. The file of the working tree is VS Code's own, which shows the pointer of a file not downloaded as it is; the **Large files** tab says which files are pointers.
@card extension lfs.download
title: Download files stored with Git LFS
place: the Files page
show: show where
Downloads the content of files left as pointers, into the working tree for the commit checked out, or into the store for another commit.
In VS Code: built: **Download** in the message of a version not downloaded sends `feature/run` of `lfs.download` with the target `lfsFiles` (protocol 1.13), the path and the commit of the version, or none for the index; once it ran, the tabs of the scheme `gitardo` said not downloaded in that repository are read again and show the content.
